1	% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
11	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
12	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
13	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
14	No overlap exists in attack templates across families.
15	The OOD benign set is IID (same generation pipeline as training); the OOD challenge is in distinguishing novel attack families from familiar benign conversations.
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
22	We also compare TF-IDF LR and LLM-as-judge baselines (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}): GPT-5.5 achieves only F1 = 0.191--0.449 on IID Crescendo with 30--47\% unparseable responses; on OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 (DD and ActorAttack) by predicting nearly all conversations as benign, and even 3-shot prompting reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (ActorAttack). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
23	
24	\paragraph{Metrics.}
25	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
26	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants.
27	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across seeds) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Bootstrap means may differ slightly from seed-averaged F1 due to the nonlinearity of F1 aggregation.
28	All standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
29	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
30	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
31	
32	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
33	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
34	\label{sec:cross_attack}
35	
36	\begin{table}[t]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted. Full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
40	\label{tab:main_results}
41	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
42	\toprule
43	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
44	\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
45	\midrule
46	9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} \\
47	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
48	Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
49	TF-IDF LR$^{\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\vspace{-2pt}
53	{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} presents the core result.
57	All methods achieve near-perfect IID F1; this reflects inherent lexical separability when test matches training.
58	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
59	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
60	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
61	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD with near-perfect per-prefix performance ($K$=1--5: 0.994--1.000); DD is thus fully solvable by lexical features alone.
62	On structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
63	Bootstrap CIs confirm non-overlapping intervals (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all variants F1\,$\geq$\,0.990; Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
64	
65	\begin{figure}[t]
66	\centering
67	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
68	\caption{OOD Deceptive Delight F1 across prefix lengths for three representative variants. Vanilla DeBERTa degrades monotonically as more (benign-appearing) attack turns accumulate. MLM maintains high F1 at early prefixes but becomes unstable at full length. The 9-class model remains stable across all prefixes.}
69	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
70	\end{figure}
71	
72	Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar} visualizes the prefix-length trajectory on OOD DD.
73	The vanilla model's monotonic degradation from $K$=1 (0.628$\pm$0.038) to full conversation (0.312$\pm$0.026) confirms that additional attack turns, each individually benign-appearing, progressively confuse a non-adapted encoder.
74	MLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.
75	The 9-class model's flat trajectory (0.972--0.997 across all prefixes) demonstrates that classification supervision stabilizes representations against this drift.
76	
77	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
78	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
79	\label{sec:decomposition}
80	
81	\begin{table}[t]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}
85	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
86	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
87	\toprule
88	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
89	\midrule
90	9-class (full model) & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
91	Binary (2-class) & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .990$\pm$.008 & \textbf{.994$\pm$.009} \\
92	Scrambled labels & .997$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
93	MLM-only (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
94	Mean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & 1.000 \\
95	\midrule
96	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
97	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
98	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
99	TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & 1.000 \\
100	\bottomrule
101	\end{tabular}
102	\vspace{-2pt}
103	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std).}
104	\end{table}
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports F1 for all variants on DD and ActorAttack. The \emph{evidence hierarchy} (ordering of variants by OOD detection performance) at full conversation length on DD is:
107	
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.31$\pm$0.03) $<$ topic (0.54$\pm$0.21) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.90$\pm$0.12) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.91$\pm$0.12) $<$ scrambled (0.997$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.997$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
110	\vspace{2pt}
111	
112	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
113	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
114	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences (all $p > 0.13$; $\leq$7 discordant pairs limit power), and paired permutation tests confirm equivalence ($p \geq 0.40$). TOST equivalence tests (margin = 5\,pp) confirm statistical equivalence on DD (all $p < 0.01$, 90\% CI within $\pm$0.05). On ActorAttack, equivalence cannot be statistically confirmed due to limited seeds ($n$=3), though observed mean differences (2--4\,pp) fall within practical equivalence margins. Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.
115	Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; bootstrap CI widths: 5.7\,pp vs.\ 35.5\,pp). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
116	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
117	
118	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
119	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
120	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
121	
122	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
123	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.
124	
125	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}
126	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
127	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
128	
129	\vspace{2pt}
130	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.26) $\approx$ vanilla (0.26$\pm$0.00) $<$ topic (0.63$\pm$0.14) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.69$\pm$0.30) $<$ jb-MLM (0.89$\pm$0.08) $<$ scrambled (0.95$\pm$0.04) $\approx$ 9-class (0.97$\pm$0.03) $\approx$ binary (0.99$\pm$0.01)}
131	\vspace{2pt}
132	
133	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
134	All classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.
135	
136	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
137	Three-seed evaluation on both DD and ActorAttack confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp on full conversations, overlapping CIs); detection thus relies on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.
138	
139	\paragraph{Summary.}
140	For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure is the primary mechanism for cross-attack detection; label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not change the detection ceiling established by domain exposure. Practitioners need continued pretraining on domain text, not annotation pipelines or specialized temporal architectures.
141	
142	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
143	\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}
144	\label{sec:cross_encoder}
145	
146	\begin{table}[t]
147	\centering
148	\small
149	\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack.}
150	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
151	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
152	\toprule
153	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
154	\midrule
155	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
156	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
157	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
158	9-class & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 \\
159	\midrule
160	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
161	Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
162	MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
163	9-class & .994$\pm$.005 & .939$\pm$.046 \\
164	\midrule
165	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
166	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
167	MLM & .884 & .874 \\
168	9-class & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
169	\bottomrule
170	\end{tabular}
171	\end{table}
172	
173	We replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
174	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack reveals this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
175	
176	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\,pp for large, +69\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\ 0.927).
177	
178	Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
179	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
180	
181	CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
182	
183	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
184	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
185	\label{sec:human_data}
186	
187	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
188	
189	\begin{table}[t]
190	\centering
191	\small
192	\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1). 3-seed mean$\pm$std. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}
193	\label{tab:human_data}
194	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
195	\toprule
196	\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\
197	\midrule
198	JB-MLM & .837$\pm$.013 \\
199	Wiki-MLM & .791$\pm$.093 \\
200	Binary & .705$\pm$.072 \\
201	Scrambled & .616$\pm$.056 \\
202	9-class & .596$\pm$.087 \\
203	Topic & .496$\pm$.042 \\
204	\midrule
205	Vanilla & .413$\pm$.021 \\
206	\bottomrule
207	\end{tabular}
208	\end{table}
209	
210	Table~\ref{tab:human_data} reports macro F1 on ToxicChat. Domain-adapted variants range from 0.596 to 0.837, while vanilla achieves 0.413. All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp, refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.
211	
212	The relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower.
213	The ranking reversal is statistically significant: JB-MLM outperforms 9-class by +24.1\,pp ($p < 0.001$, permutation test; 95\% CI [0.816, 0.858] vs.\ [0.569, 0.624], non-overlapping), and Wiki-MLM outperforms 9-class by +20.1\,pp ($p < 0.001$).
214	JB-MLM exhibits the lowest cross-seed variance (std = 0.013 vs.\ 0.087 for 9-class). Unsupervised domain adaptation thus produces the most stable representations on out-of-distribution human data.
215	ToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer to single-turn detection without relying on sequential patterns. This is a modality mismatch, not a finding about variant quality: the multi-turn pipeline's full evidence hierarchy requires multi-turn human data to validate.
216	
217	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
218	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
219	\label{sec:perturbation}
220	
221	Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20), classification-supervised variants achieve zero F1 degradation at full conversation length on IID and OOD DD, while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp (McNemar 9-class vs.\ vanilla $p < 0.001$ on OOD DD).
222	On ActorAttack, absolute robustness does not hold (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\ non-adapted gap (9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248, $p < 0.001$).
223	Absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent; DAPT's detection advantage persists under paraphrasing.
224	Full results in Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}.
225	
226	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
227	\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
228	\label{sec:strategy}
229	
230	The 9-class persuasion taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families ($\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Section~\ref{sec:method}).
231	Transition count matrices reveal distinct orchestration patterns: Crescendo cycles broadly across all strategy categories, FITD concentrates on Role$\to$Obfuscation, and DD on Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}).
232	On unseen attacks, strategy prediction degrades to near-random ($\kappa$=0.126 on OOD DD), consistent with the detection mechanism analysis: all classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection F1 (0.997--1.000) regardless of strategy prediction quality. Detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.
233

Full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{\\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\\\\n\\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & .981$\\pm$.013 & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} \\\\\nMLM-only$^{\\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \\textbf{.993$\\pm$.009} & .914$\\pm$.115 \\\\\nVanilla & .978$\\pm$.016 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 & .312$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the core result.\nAll methods achieve near-perfect IID F1; this reflects inherent lexical separability when test matches training.\nOn OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.\nMLM achieves mean F1 $\\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \\leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.\nAt full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.997$\\pm$0.005.\nTF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD with near-perfect per-prefix performance ($K$=1--5: 0.994--1.000); DD is thus fully solvable by lexical features alone.\nOn structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\\pm$0.026: a 71\\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nBootstrap CIs confirm non-overlapping intervals (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990; Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}\n\\caption{OOD Deceptive Delight F1 across prefix lengths for three representative variants. Vanilla DeBERTa degrades monotonically as more (benign-appearing) attack turns accumulate. MLM maintains high F1 at early prefixes but becomes unstable at full length. The 9-class model remains stable across all prefixes.}\n\\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar} visualizes the prefix-length trajectory on OOD DD.\nThe vanilla model's monotonic degradation from $K$=1 (0.628$\\pm$0.038) to full conversation (0.312$\\pm$0.026) confirms that additional attack turns, each individually benign-appearing, progressively confuse a non-adapted encoder.\nMLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.\nThe 9-class model's flat trajectory (0.972--0.997 across all prefixes) demonstrates that classification supervision stabilizes representations against this drift.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}\n\\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (full model) & .997$\\pm$.005 & .972$\\pm$.026 & .981$\\pm$.013 \\\\\nBinary (2-class) & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .990$\\pm$.008 & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.009} \\\\\nScrambled labels & .997$\\pm$.005 & .951$\\pm$.043 & .981$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nMLM-only (no labels) & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 & .993$\\pm$.009 \\\\\nMean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\\pm$.013 & .962$\\pm$.015 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTopic classif.\\ (5-class) & .541$\\pm$.207 & .625$\\pm$.141 & .593$\\pm$.166 \\\\\nWikipedia MLM & .904$\\pm$.115 & .686$\\pm$.297 & .911$\\pm$.073 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\\dagger}$Deterministic (no std).}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports F1 for all variants on DD and ActorAttack. The \\emph{evidence hierarchy} (ordering of variants by OOD detection performance) at full conversation length on DD is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small vanilla (0.31$\\pm$0.03) $<$ topic (0.54$\\pm$0.21) $\\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.90$\\pm$0.12) $\\approx$ jb-MLM (0.91$\\pm$0.12) $<$ scrambled (0.997$\\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.997$\\pm$0.005) $\\approx$ binary (1.000$\\pm$0.000)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}\nMLM bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences (all $p > 0.13$; $\\leq$7 discordant pairs limit power), and paired permutation tests confirm equivalence ($p \\geq 0.40$). TOST equivalence tests (margin = 5\\,pp) confirm statistical equivalence on DD (all $p < 0.01$, 90\\% CI within $\\pm$0.05). On ActorAttack, equivalence cannot be statistically confirmed due to limited seeds ($n$=3), though observed mean differences (2--4\\,pp) fall within practical equivalence margins. Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.\nClassification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; bootstrap CI widths: 5.7\\,pp vs.\\ 35.5\\,pp). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\nAppendix~\\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.26) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.26$\\pm$0.00) $<$ topic (0.63$\\pm$0.14) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.69$\\pm$0.30) $<$ jb-MLM (0.89$\\pm$0.08) $<$ scrambled (0.95$\\pm$0.04) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.97$\\pm$0.03) $\\approx$ binary (0.99$\\pm$0.01)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nAll classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nThree-seed evaluation on both DD and ActorAttack confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp on full conversations, overlapping CIs); detection thus relies on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nFor base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure is the primary mechanism for cross-attack detection; label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but does not change the detection ceiling established by domain exposure. Practitioners need continued pretraining on domain text, not annotation pipelines or specialized temporal architectures.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & .997$\\pm$.005 & .972$\\pm$.026 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & .994$\\pm$.005 & .939$\\pm$.046 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884 & .874 \\\\\n9-class & .987$\\pm$.004 & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack reveals this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\\ 0.927).\n\nThree encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nClassification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\\ 0.026--0.201).\n\nCKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1). 3-seed mean$\\pm$std. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & .837$\\pm$.013 \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:human_data} reports macro F1 on ToxicChat. Domain-adapted variants range from 0.596 to 0.837, while vanilla achieves 0.413. All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp, refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n\nThe relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower.\nThe ranking reversal is statistically significant: JB-MLM outperforms 9-class by +24.1\\,pp ($p < 0.001$, permutation test; 95\\% CI [0.816, 0.858] vs.\\ [0.569, 0.624], non-overlapping), and Wiki-MLM outperforms 9-class by +20.1\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).\nJB-MLM exhibits the lowest cross-seed variance (std = 0.013 vs.\\ 0.087 for 9-class). Unsupervised domain adaptation thus produces the most stable representations on out-of-distribution human data.\nToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer to single-turn detection without relying on sequential patterns. This is a modality mismatch, not a finding about variant quality: the multi-turn pipeline's full evidence hierarchy requires multi-turn human data to validate.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20), classification-supervised variants achieve zero F1 degradation at full conversation length on IID and OOD DD, while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp (McNemar 9-class vs.\\ vanilla $p < 0.001$ on OOD DD).\nOn ActorAttack, absolute robustness does not hold (9-class $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4$\\pm$13.5\\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\\ non-adapted gap (9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\\ vanilla 0.248, $p < 0.001$).\nAbsolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent; DAPT's detection advantage persists under paraphrasing.\nFull results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}\n\\label{sec:strategy}\n\nThe 9-class persuasion taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families ($\\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Section~\\ref{sec:method}).\nTransition count matrices reveal distinct orchestration patterns: Crescendo cycles broadly across all strategy categories, FITD concentrates on Role$\\to$Obfuscation, and DD on Emotion$\\to$Obfuscation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}).\nOn unseen attacks, strategy prediction degrades to near-random ($\\kappa$=0.126 on OOD DD), consistent with the detection mechanism analysis: all classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection F1 (0.997--1.000) regardless of strategy prediction quality. Detection operates through domain-adapted \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.\n",

1	% related_work.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	\label{sec:related_work}
5	
6	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
7	Multi-turn attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} implements gradual escalation, FITD~\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering, PAIR~\citep{chao2023pair} and TAP~\citep{mehrotra2024tap} automate iterative refinement. \citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy, and standardized benchmarks~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} enable systematic comparison. A shared reliance on persuasion dynamics~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj} distinguishes these from token-level attacks such as GCG~\citep{zou2023gcg}.
8	
9	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
10	Existing defenses model conversation-level structure but do not decompose what enables cross-attack generalization. DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU on a single attack family, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies GNN classification, and SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026} predicts unsafe continuations. None evaluate cross-family OOD transfer. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model multi-turn manipulation trajectories; perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.
11	
12	\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}
13	RST~\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, dialogue acts~\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact}, and Speech Act Theory~\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} model pragmatic structure. PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques grounded in Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Related work in propaganda analysis~\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\citep{wang2019persuasion} uses technique-level classification to capture manipulative intent. Our ablation reveals that the classification taxonomy does not function as a detection mechanism; detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.
14	
15	\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}
16	ULMFiT~\citep{howard2018ulmfit} established pretrain-then-fine-tune; \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} showed continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains. STILTs~\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} use intermediate tasks. Our MLM control confirms that domain pretraining bridges the OOD gap without classification labels. Dataset artifact studies~\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing}, adversarial NLP attacks~\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive}, and probing methods~\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} inform our TF-IDF baseline analysis and perturbation evaluation.
17	
```

30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.

127	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior on IID data.
128	Classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length regardless of label correctness.
129	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp.
130	McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (all $p > 0.13$), though with $\leq$7 discordant pairs, statistical power is limited.

154	On OOD DD, the IID pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs).
155	On OOD ActorAttack, domain-adapted variants degrade substantially (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, MLM $-$35.3\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\ non-adapted gap: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$).
156	Domain specificity also manifests: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.

158	\section{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
159	\label{sec:strategy_full}
160	
161	\begin{figure*}[t]
162	\centering
163	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}
164	\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Strategy labels validated on $n$=50 samples with a single annotator ($\kappa$=0.78); fine-grained trajectory patterns should be interpreted with this limitation. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}
165	\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}
166	\end{figure*}
167	
168	Figure~\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack types.
169	Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.
170	FITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\citep{cialdini2001influence}.
171	DD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.

JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.

214	\vspace{-2pt}
215	{\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.97.}
216	\end{table}
217	
218	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.
219	
220	\begin{table}[h]
221	\centering
222	\footnotesize
223	\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}
224	\label{tab:cross_llm_error}
225	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
226	\toprule
227	\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
228	\midrule
229	TP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\% & 68.0\% & 100.0\% \\
230	FP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\% & 88.0\% & 90.0\% \\
231	FP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\% & 0.9\% & 0.0\% \\
232	\bottomrule
233	\end{tabular}
234	\end{table}
235	
236	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\% on Llama vs.\ 0--0.9\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; multi-LLM training data would address this confound directly.
237	
238	\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}
239	\label{app:roberta_large_lr}
240	
241	\begin{table}[h]
242	\centering
243	\footnotesize
244	\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}
245	\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}
246	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
247	\toprule
248	\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\
249	\midrule
250	Vanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\pm$.036 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
251	9-class, LR=$2 \times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
252	9-class, LR=$1 \times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\pm$.034 & .781$\pm$.054 \\
253	\bottomrule
254	\end{tabular}
255	\end{table}
256	
257	Reducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874), confirming that representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.
258	
259	\section{Reproducibility Details}
260	\label{sec:reproducibility}
261	
262	\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}
263	All variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).
264	
265	\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}
266	The BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \to 256 \to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.
267	
268	\paragraph{Data generation.}
269	Jailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.
270	
271	\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}
272	Each user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.
273	
274	\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}
275	The paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.
276	
```

</details>

Training uses learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}\nEach user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}\nThe paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 276
    }

## Phase 2: Chapter-by-chapter reflection

### 1. Abstract
Current state: 7 sentences, ~150 words. Good narrative structure with "The answer is reductive" hook.

Strengths:
- Clear problem statement
- "The answer is reductive" is a bold, memorable hook
- Numbers are specific and meaningful

Weaknesses:
- "yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear" — too generic


- Sentence 2 packs too much methodological detail when it should draw readers in
- The ToxicChat reference at the end feels disconnected from the main narrative
- "domain exposure alone bridges ~60 percentage points" is compelling, but the abstract doesn't explain why this matters beyond the paper itself

### 2. Introduction
Current state: Well-structured 5-paragraph + contribution list. Contains hypothesis-breaking narrative.

Strengths:
- Good "simplest hypothesis fails" framing
- Specific numbers to back up claims
- Clear contribution list with section references

Weaknesses:
- Opening paragraph starts with "Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose..." — decent but not grabbing
- The second paragraph about related work feels premature before establishing the core question
- The hypothesis failure section is the strongest part but comes too late
- "The open question is: what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?" — this is the real hook and should surface earlier
- First contribution item runs too long — needs tightening
- "Several counter-intuitive negative results" is vague — should specify what they are
- Some phrasing could be more direct: "qualitatively different threat" works, but "distinguishes these from" is unnecessarily formal

### 3. Related Work
Current state: 4 paragraphs, very compressed.

Strengths:
- Good thematic organization into 4 clusters
- Each paragraph positions our work relative to existing literature
- Covers the landscape comprehensively

Weaknesses:
- Reads too much like a citation list in places — needs more connective tissue
- "Existing def..." — this sentence cuts off, but the section overall feels rushed
- Long citation strings without synthesis
- Missing a clear gap statement that ties back to what we're actually contributing

### 4. Method
Current state: Clean, well-organized. Table 1 is good.

Strengths:
- Table 1 effectively summarizes 7 variants
- Frozen encoder design justification is clear
- Math notation is minimal and appropriate

Weaknesses:
- "DisPeD is a two-stage framework" — opening feels flat
- The 9-class variant description takes up a whole paragraph about taxonomy — too much space
- Remaining variants section is well-structured but reads like a list
- Missing: why THIS set of ablation axes? What's the design philosophy?

### 5. Experiments
Current state: Longest section, well-organized into...

Strengths:
- Evidence hierarchy display is visually effective
- Good use of bootstrap CIs and statistical tests
- Cross-encoder validation is compelling
- Human data section is honest about limitations

Weaknesses:
- Setup paragraph is very dense — lots of numbers without narrative framing
- "Table X reports..." pattern repeats many times — could vary sentence openings
- §4.2 paragraph structure could flow better — currently reads as "finding 1, finding 2, finding 3"
- The evidence hierarchy display uses $\approx$, $<$, $\ll$ — effective but could have a brief interpretive sentence after each
- §4.4 representation drift paragraph is well-written
- Strategy section feels somewhat disconnected from the main narrative

### 6. Discussion
Current state: 4 short paragraphs addressing confounds.

Strengths: Organized around specific confounds with each paragraph tackling a distinct question, and the limitations are presented honestly.

Weaknesses: The writing feels too terse and could benefit from better transitions between ideas. The variance reversal finding on human data deserves more exploration rather than being glossed over, and the overall structure reads more like a checklist than a flowing argument.

### 7. Conclusion
Current state: 3 sentences. Very compressed.

Strengths: The three main findings are clearly laid out and the future work is concrete.

Weaknesses: Feels underdeveloped and hurried. The opening "yields three findings" is generic, and there's no sense of what these results mean for the broader field or research community.

### 8. Limitations
Current state: 7 paragraphs, thorough.

Strengths: Detailed and transparent about constraints, with particularly strong analysis of cross-LLM generalization and computational accessibility.

Weaknesses: Some sections run too long and could be tightened, particularly the dataset scale discussion.

---

For Phase 3, I'm focusing on: sharpening the Abstract's hook and making ToxicChat feel integral rather than tacked on; opening the Introduction with more punch and moving the representation question earlier while trimming the contribution list; adding synthesis to Related Work instead of just listing citations; motivating the Method section better and clarifying the ablation philosophy; and varying how Experiments paragraphs begin to improve narrative flow.

I also need to tighten the Discussion so it reads as one coherent argument, strengthen the Conclusion's opening and add broader implications, and compress Limitations while keeping the honesty intact. All of this within 8 pages, preserving every number and core claim, avoiding trailing -ing phrases, em-dashes, and blacklisted words.

For the Abstract, I'm condensing the core finding: multi-turn jailbreak detection depends heavily on domain exposure for smaller models, but this requirement disappears at scale. The ablation spans three model sizes and six pretraining dimensions across multiple attack families. I'll tighten this to emphasize the key insight without the granular experimental details.

Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns, yet we don't fully understand how pretrained encoders learn to detect this pattern. Through systematic ablation of encoder variants at different scales and pretraining approaches, we find the answer hinges on model capacity: smaller encoders (125–184M) need domain-specific pretraining to close the gap on out-of-distribution attacks, but scaling to 355M parameters eliminates this requirement entirely. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within synthetic data but doesn't transfer to human-written attacks, where domain-adaptive pretraining on synthetic jailbreaks does transfer effectively to real ToxicChat examples. , defeating per-turn safety filters. What representation properties enable a detector to generalize across unseen attack families?

I'm decomposing this through controlled ablation: twelve encoder variants across three scales (125M, 184M, 355M parameters) and six adaptation dimensions, tested on three out-of-distribution attack families.

The findings are striking. For base-size models, domain exposure alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD performance gap, while label quality, granularity, and temporal modeling barely matter. Scaling to 355M parameters removes the need for domain adaptation on structurally different attacks entirely—achieving perfect F1 on ActorAttack without any adaptation—which suggests a clear capacity gradient where larger models need less specialized training. Classification supervision does stabilize in-distribution predictions substantially, reducing variance by 4-20x, but this benefit doesn't carry over to human-written data, where masked language modeling actually outperforms supervised classification approaches.

Testing on ToxicChat shows that domain-adaptive pretraining on synthetic text does transfer to real jailbreaks, reaching 0.837 macro F1 compared to 0.413 for the baseline, though full multi-turn human evaluation is still ahead. I'm refining the abstract to be more concrete about the problem—multi-turn attacks distribute harmful intent across individually benign turns, which defeats per-turn filters. The research question should ask directly what representation properties enable generalization across unseen attack families. For the second sentence, I'm simplifying the methods description by removing redundant details about Crescendo and tightening the language around "adaptation dimensions" instead of "pretraining dimensions." Then I'll move into the introduction to compress the contributions and name the counter-intuitive findings rather than leaving them vague. Now I'm refining the remaining sentences. For sentence 7, I want to integrate ToxicChat more naturally into the narrative and acknowledge that full multi-turn evaluation is still ahead. The page budget is extremely tight, so I need to be strategic about what stays and what gets compressed further.

Looking at the overall structure, the Abstract and Introduction are where I can make the most visible improvements without risking the layout. The Experiments section is the longest and where I might find 2-3 lines to save. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WYtHrCizhsHizzziN7CSvT`</summary>

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.
7	
8	Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\,=\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.
11	
12	We address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.
13	
14	Three principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\,=\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp), with 4--20$\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

      "content": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \\emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 24,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 24
    }
  },
