    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation).",
    "newString": "A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation\\footnote{This setup is inspired by but does not replicate DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function.} collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation).",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. The open question remains: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization, and why do some adaptation strategies transfer while others fail?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

          "   \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          "+On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).",
          " ",
          " RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\\ 0.927).",

Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.",
  "new_string": "The central question of this paper requires a controlled experimental design: each candidate factor for cross-attack generalization must be isolated while all other variables are held constant. DisPeD achieves this through a two-stage architecture. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder; seven Stage-1 variants decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification under identical conditions for all variants. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline."
}

    "oldString": "DisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.",
    "newString": "The central question of this paper requires a controlled experimental design: each candidate factor for cross-attack generalization must be isolated while all other variables are held constant. DisPeD achieves this through a two-stage architecture. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder; seven Stage-1 variants decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification under identical conditions for all variants. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.",
    "originalFile": "% method.tex — D024 compressed\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nDisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe remaining classification variants test specific ablation hypotheses.\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant assigns random permutations of the 9-class labels to turns, preserving the classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics; this isolates whether the classification objective itself provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\\ general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{512}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($512 \\to 256 \\to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nThree-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n",

          "-DisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013).   "new_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe evidence hierarchy is robust to several potential confounds, though important caveats remain.\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). This pattern suggests that classification supervision overfits to distributional regularities in the training data, while MLM captures more transferable token-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions."

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions, while MLM generalizes across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows.     "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe evidence hierarchy is robust to several potential confounds, though important caveats remain.\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). This pattern suggests that classification supervision overfits to distributional regularities in the training data, while MLM captures more transferable token-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nExisting defenses model conversation-level structure but do not decompose what enables cross-attack generalization. DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU on a single attack family, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies GNN classification, and SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026} predicts unsafe continuations. None evaluate cross-family OOD transfer. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model multi-turn manipulation trajectories; perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nExisting defenses model conversation-level structure through varied architectures: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies GNN classification, and SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026} predicts unsafe continuations. Each reports in-distribution results on a single attack family; none evaluate cross-family OOD transfer or analyze what drives generalization. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model multi-turn manipulation trajectories; perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks. Our contribution fills this gap: a systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization."
}

< % abstract.tex — D046 deep writing upgrade

> % abstract.tex — R3 revision
3,10c3,10
< Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns, defeating per-turn safety filters.
< What representation properties enable a detector to generalize across unseen attack families?
< We isolate the answer through controlled ablation: twelve encoder variants spanning three scales (125M, 184M, 355M) and six adaptation dimensions, evaluated on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families.
< The answer is reductive.
< For base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), domain exposure alone bridges $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD F1 gap; label quality, label granularity, and temporal modeling are non-factors.
< Scaling to 355M parameters eliminates the need for adaptation on structurally distinct attacks (F1\,=\,1.000 on ActorAttack without any continued pretraining), revealing a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring adaptation narrows.
< Classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where pure masked language modeling outperforms all classification variants.
< Validation on ToxicChat confirms that domain-adaptive pretraining on synthetic text transfers to human-written jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.
---
> Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.
> What representation properties enable detection that generalizes across attack families?
> We address this through a controlled ablation on synthetic multi-turn evaluation data: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3 and three on RoBERTa, evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo, decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.
> Three findings emerge.
> First, domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels; TF-IDF collapses on structurally complex ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.261) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
> Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical, though capacity differences (184M vs.\ 125M parameters) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method.
> Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
> DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.
FILES DIFFER

6c6
< Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.
---
> Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind.
8c8

=== related_work.tex ===

=== method.tex ===
6c6
< The central question of this paper requires a controlled experimental design: each candidate factor for cross-attack generalization must be isolated while all other variables are held constant. DisPeD achieves this through a two-stage architecture. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder; seven Stage-1 variants decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification under identical conditions for all variants. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.
---
> DisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.
10c10

< All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences (all $p > 0.13$; $\leq$7 discordant pairs limit power), and paired permutation tests confirm equivalence ($p \geq 0.40$). Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.

< 
< \paragraph{Summary.}
< For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure is the primary mechanism for cross-attack detection; label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not change the detection ceiling established by domain exposure. Practitioners need continued pretraining on domain text, not annotation pipelines or specialized temporal architectures.
---
> Mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 = 1.000 on DD, single seed), suggesting detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential order. Multi-seed validation is needed (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).

< To test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
< On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
< 
< RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\,pp for large, +69\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\ 0.927).
---
> To test whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes beyond DeBERTa-v3, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (full results in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}, Appendix~\ref{sec:cross_encoder_full}).
> On DD, RoBERTa vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so continued pretraining adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack reveals that this apparent sufficiency reflects DD's simplicity: RoBERTa vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop. Domain-adapted RoBERTa recovers (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
178,179c147,148
< Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
< Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
---
> Encoder pretraining method determines the \emph{difficulty threshold} at which adaptation becomes critical: DeBERTa's RTD pretraining~\citep{he2023debertav3,clark2020electra} requires adaptation even for simple attacks, while RoBERTa's MLM pretraining~\citep{liu2019roberta} tolerates simple attacks but fails on structurally complex ones. Bootstrap CIs confirm this gap on DD: RoBERTa vanilla 95\% CI [0.883, 1.000] vs.\ DeBERTa vanilla [0.202, 0.341], no overlap.
> Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both encoders (cross-seed variance drops to 0.005--0.046 from 0.026--0.201).
181c150,151
< CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
---
> Centered CKA~\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape representations on both encoders, yet comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains depending on the starting point (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
> Linear probing on diagnostic tasks shows 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\,pp; MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.
187c157
< Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
---
> A key concern is whether the evidence hierarchy reflects genuine beyond-lexical generalization or merely within-LLM stylistic transfer, since both training and OOD evaluation data are generated by Qwen3-8B. We test this by evaluating on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat}, a dataset of 204 human-written jailbreak attempts and 204 benign conversations collected from real LMSYS user interactions, entirely independent of any LLM generation pipeline.
210c180
< All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}), refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.
---
> Table~\ref{tab:human_data} reports macro F1 on ToxicChat. Domain-adapted variants range from 0.596 to 0.837, while vanilla achieves 0.413. All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp, refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.
212,215c182
< The relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower.
< The ranking reversal is statistically significant: JB-MLM outperforms 9-class by +24.1\,pp ($p < 0.001$, permutation test; 95\% CI [0.816, 0.858] vs.\ [0.569, 0.624], non-overlapping), and Wiki-MLM outperforms 9-class by +20.1\,pp ($p < 0.001$).
< JB-MLM exhibits the lowest cross-seed variance (std = 0.013 vs.\ 0.087 for 9-class). Unsupervised domain adaptation thus produces the most stable representations on out-of-distribution human data.
< ToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer to single-turn detection without relying on sequential patterns. This is a modality mismatch, not a finding about variant quality: the multi-turn pipeline's full evidence hierarchy requires multi-turn human data to validate.
---
> The relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower. ToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer to single-turn detection without relying on sequential patterns. This is a modality mismatch, not a finding about variant quality: the multi-turn pipeline's full evidence hierarchy requires multi-turn human data to validate.

< Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20), classification-supervised variants achieve zero F1 degradation at full conversation length on IID and OOD DD, while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp (McNemar 9-class vs.\ vanilla $p < 0.001$ on OOD DD).
---
> Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20), DAPT's detection advantage persists.
> On IID and OOD DD, classification-supervised variants achieve zero F1 degradation at full conversation length, while non-adapted models (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp (McNemar 9-class vs.\ vanilla $p < 0.001$ on OOD DD).
223d190
< Absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent; DAPT's detection advantage persists under paraphrasing.
232c199
< On unseen attacks, strategy prediction degrades to near-random ($\kappa$=0.126 on OOD DD), consistent with the detection mechanism analysis: all classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection F1 (0.997--1.000) regardless of strategy prediction quality. Detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.
---
> On unseen attacks, strategy prediction degrades to near-random ($\kappa$=0.126 on OOD DD), consistent with the detection mechanism analysis: all classification-supervised variants achieve identical detection F1 (0.997$\pm$0.005) regardless of strategy prediction quality. Detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.

On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\\ 0.927).\n---\n> To test whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes beyond DeBERTa-v3, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (full results in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder_full}).\n> On DD, RoBERTa vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so continued pretraining adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack reveals that this apparent sufficiency reflects DD's simplicity: RoBERTa vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop. Domain-adapted RoBERTa recovers (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n178,179c147,148\n< Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n< Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\\ 0.026--0.201).\n---\n> Encoder pretraining method determines the \\emph{difficulty threshold} at which adaptation becomes critical: DeBERTa's RTD pretraining~\\citep{he2023debertav3,clark2020electra} requires adaptation even for simple attacks, while RoBERTa's MLM pretraining~\\citep{liu2019roberta} tolerates simple attacks but fails on structurally complex ones. Bootstrap CIs confirm this gap on DD: RoBERTa vanilla 95\\% CI [0.883, 1.000] vs.\\ DeBERTa vanilla [0.202, 0.341], no overlap.\n> Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both encoders (cross-seed variance drops to 0.005--0.046 from 0.026--0.201).\n181c150,151\n< CKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n---\n> Centered CKA~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape representations on both encoders, yet comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains depending on the starting point (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n> Linear probing on diagnostic tasks shows 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\\,pp; MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.\n187c157\n< Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n---\n> A key concern is whether the evidence hierarchy reflects genuine beyond-lexical generalization or merely within-LLM stylistic transfer, since both training and OOD evaluation data are generated by Qwen3-8B. We test this by evaluating on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat}, a dataset of 204 human-written jailbreak attempts and 204 benign conversations collected from real LMSYS user interactions, entirely independent of any LLM generation pipeline.\n210c180\n< All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n---\n> Table~\\ref{tab:human_data} reports macro F1 on ToxicChat. Domain-adapted variants range from 0.596 to 0.837, while vanilla achieves 0.413. All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp, refuting the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n212,215c182\n< The relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower.\n< The ranking reversal is statistically significant: JB-MLM outperforms 9-class by +24.1\\,pp ($p < 0.001$, permutation test; 95\\% CI [0.816, 0.858] vs.\\ [0.569, 0.624], non-overlapping), and Wiki-MLM outperforms 9-class by +20.1\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).\n< JB-MLM exhibits the lowest cross-seed variance (std = 0.013 vs.\\ 0.087 for 9-class). Unsupervised domain adaptation thus produces the most stable representations on out-of-distribution human data.\n< ToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer to single-turn detection without relying on sequential patterns. This is a modality mismatch, not a finding about variant quality: the multi-turn pipeline's full evidence hierarchy requires multi-turn human data to validate.\n---\n> The relative ordering among domain-adapted variants differs from multi-turn evaluations: JB-MLM leads while classification-supervised variants rank lower. ToxicChat contains single-turn direct attacks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's shaping of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UEimh3rnrb9y9PPBFNMggL`</summary>

```text
8	Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. The open question remains: \emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization, and why do some adaptation strategies transfer while others fail?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation\footnote{This setup is inspired by but does not replicate DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function.} collapses from F1\,=\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.
11	
12	We address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.
13	
14	Three principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\,=\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity (binary vs.\ 9-class) does not affect detection.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp), with 4--20$\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
```

  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "86fbb9e6-3cf7-4a89-8b0e-80c55179b62d",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
      "content": "Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. The open question remains: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization, and why do some adaptation strategies transfer while others fail?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation\\footnote{This setup is inspired by but does not replicate DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function.} collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity (binary vs.\\ 9-class) does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).",

    }

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. ```text
1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	The evidence hierarchy is robust to several potential confounds, though important caveats remain.
7	
8	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
9	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.
10	
11	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
12	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.
13	
14	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
15	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ JB-MLM 0.013). This pattern suggests that classification supervision overfits to distributional regularities in the training data, while MLM captures more transferable token-level features.
16	
17	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
18	DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions.
19	
20	\section{Conclusion}
21	\label{sec:conclusion}
22	
23	Our ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not transfer to human data. The practical implication is that practitioners building cross-attack detectors should invest in domain-adaptive pretraining and encoder capacity rather than annotation pipelines. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.
24	
25	\section{Limitations}
26	\label{sec:limitations}
27	
28	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
29	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
30	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
31	
32	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
33	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
34	
35	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
36	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
37	
38	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
39	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
40	
41	\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}
42	FITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.
43	
44	\paragraph{Scope.}
45	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
46	
47	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
48	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
49	
```

</details>
